    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\n\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time. There's a tension between the metrics showing genuine_count: 0 and the taxonomy_note claiming "genuine=3" for s44. The metrics note explains that D091 identified 3 samples escaping text-match classifiers but marked them as functionally degenerate with ISR=0, while the taxonomy_note lists these as H1-near samples. The distinction appears to be that genuine_strict=0, meaning none qualify as genuinely novel under the stricter criteria. So the ROADMAP's 1.0%±1.7% figure comes from counting s44's three H1-near samples as genuine using the non-strict definition, which gives me 0%, 0%, 3% across the samples—averaging to exactly 1.0% with a standard deviation around 1.73%. But D091 marked these as functionally degenerate since they rely on bypass techniques rather than genuine novelty, which is why the metrics field shows genuine_count=0 under the stricter interpretation. I'm seeing there's a distinction between genuine=3 (the classifier's output including H1-near cases) and genuine_strict=0 (after manual review filtering those out), and the metrics field uses the strict count while the ROADMAP cites the non-strict percentage—this mismatch needs to be flagged. Now I should focus on what actually matters for auditing this task: verifying the classifier output matches the registry metrics and checking the H1 counts align.

The genuine count dropped from 7 to 0, which seems like a significant shift worth validating. But there's a problem—the checkpoint directory where the eval data should be stored no longer exists on disk, so I need to figure out where the classify_h7 script actually ran its evaluation against and whether those results are still accessible elsewhere. I've already established that the checkpoint directory is missing and the classify tool ran today on potentially stale data. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can see the actual proof output for the first sample. This is a clear case of H1 (conclusion embedded in premises):

- Premises include `P(a)` and `¬P(a)` (contradiction), along with many repeated `P(a) → Q(a)` premises
- Conclusion is `P(a) → Q(a)` which is literally one of the premises
- Also there's a contradiction (P(a) and ¬P(a)) making anything provable

This is clearly not a genuine proof. The model has:


1. Embedded the conclusion directly in the premises (H1 pattern)
2. Added a contradiction for good measure
3. Padded with many irrelevant premises

The classify tool correctly identifies this as H1. Now I'm cross-checking the metrics between the eval data and the registry to make sure everything's consistent - the prove_rate, n_proved, and n_problems all match up at 0.96, 96, and 100 respectively.

Let me spot-check the ISR values and a few more samples to confirm the H1 classification holds, and I should also verify that the GPU constraints are set correctly for the running experiments.
```

Verification results:
- `execution_reward: 1.0` appears 96 times → 96 proved, consistent with n_proved=96 ✅
- `sta_reward: 0.0` appears 100 times → ISR=0 for all 100 samples. Wait, that can't be right — the registry says mean_isr=0.0027, not 0. Let me think... actually the 4 non-proved samples would also have sta_reward=0, so 96 proved with some ISR>0 + 4 not proved with ISR=0 could still give 100 counts of sta_reward=0.0. 

But I'm realizing the grep pattern might be matching "sta_reward": 0.0 as a substring, which would catch values like 0.0588 too. So the 100 count likely means every sample has a sta_reward field starting with 0.0, which would be consistent with the mean_isr value if most samples have very small ISR values.

Now I'm checking the GPU constraints for the running experiments by looking at the PIDs and their assigned GPUs to verify which experiments are actually running. The PIDs are close enough to suggest child processes spawned during execution, which is normal behavior. Now I need to check the project's execution config to see which GPUs are actually allowed for these jobs.

D001\\n- id: D003\\n  date: 2026-05-13 20:50 UTC\\n  summary: Phase 1 r4 fast_isr_fol 训练信号无效，改用 full STA + K=8 重跑\\n  rationale: fast_isr_fol 是二值 O(1) 近似（ISR=0 tautology / ISR=1 non-tautology），Qwen3-4B\\n    生成的 proved formalization 几乎全部 ISR=1，与 execution-only 零差异。full STA 提供连续 ISR（0.125-0.889\\n    in Phase 0），能给出真实梯度信号。K=4 导致 frac_reward_zero_std=0.5-1.0（GRPO 无 advantage），K=8\\n    增加 within-group variance。两个改动分别解决 STA 信号粒度和 GRPO 梯度信号两个独立问题。\\n  related_experiments:\\n  - exp_id: sta-phase1-exec-only-r4\\n    role: evidence\\n    result: prove_rate 12%→15%, ISR ~0.15, minimal learning\\n  - exp_id: sta-phase1-exec-sta-r4\\n    role: evidence\\n    result: 与 exec-only 几乎完全相同, fast_isr 提供零额外信号\\n  depends_on:\\n  - D002\\n- id: D004\\n  date: 2026-05-13 21:59 UTC\\n  summary: Reviewer 指出 exec-sta-r5 prove_rate 低于 baseline（退化），STA 50/50 权重过于惩罚性。立即补跑低权重\\n    + bonus 模式实验\\n  rationale: exec-sta-r5 prove_rate=0.10 < baseline=0.12 < exec-only=0.15。连续 ISR 给\\n    proved-but-low-ISR 样本 reward≈0.3（几乎等于未 prove），模型学到 prove 不值得。Kill criteria 未触发（差\\n    5pp 非 15pp，权重未调整），但核心假设受挑战。需立即验证：(1) sta_weight=0.1 降低惩罚 (2) ISR-as-bonus 模式 r=exec×(1+α×ISR)\\n    避免惩罚。\\n  related_experiments:\\n  - exp_id: sta-phase1-exec-sta-r5\\n    role: evidence\\n    result: prove_rate=0.10 < baseline, STA reward 过于惩罚性\\n  - exp_id: sta-phase1-exec-only-r5\\n    role: control\\n    result: prove_rate=0.15, 复现 r4\\n  depends_on:\\n  - D003\\n- id: D005\\n  date: 2026-05-13 22:13 UTC\\n  summary: ISR/PR 下降因果归因待验证，plan_003 提升为最高优先级消歧实验\\n  rationale: Reviewer 指出 ISR/PR 64%→33% 有两种解释：(A) STA 检测到 hacking (B) 50/50 权重抑制 prove\\n    行为的惩罚效应。ISR 绝对值 ~0.05 ≈ pretrained 水平支持解释 B。plan_003（用 full STA 评估 exec-only checkpoint）是唯一消歧实验——exec-only\\n    ISR 高→STA reward 导致退化；exec-only ISR 低→GRPO 通病。paper_framing.md 已将 claim 降级为观察性表述，因果表述需等\\n    plan_003 结果。cuda:1 排列改为 plan_003 eval 先行，再 plan_005 training。\\n  related_experiments:\\n  - PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\n\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time. ```text
The worker name is `worker_code_tex-isr-correction-c`, which suggests it was making corrections to the TeX files related to ISR values. I see that:

1. `experiments.tex` was modified at 01:10 (UTC+8, so ~17:10 UTC on May 15)
2. `appendix.tex` was modified at 01:20 (UTC+8, so ~17:20 UTC)
3. `introduction.tex` was modified at 01:10
4. `.bak` files were created for experiments.tex and appendix.tex at 00:52


5. Looking at the registry updates for `plan001-exec-only-s43-1000-r2`, I can see new ISR-related fields have been added: the mean ISR is now recorded as 0.531, with a standard deviation of 0.15, and a corrected ISR value of 0.465, along with detailed artifact notes explaining the sources of the discrepancy.

The worker's annotation reveals that s43's ISR is inflated due to H7a tautological proofs where quantifier decomposition artificially inflates the component count, making everything appear "necessary" in a trivial sense. The corrected ISR of 0.465 is more meaningful, though I'm realizing that for purely tautological experiments, ISR itself may not be a useful quality metric since every component is technically necessary by definition.

I should check the actual TeX file to see what ISR values are being reported in the paper and compare them against the backup version to understand what corrections were made.
```

PR rises 32\\\\%$\\\\to$93\\\\%$\\\\to$96\\\\% while proved-only ISR falls $0.642{\\\\to}0.132{\\\\to}0.003$ over 100$\\\\to$500$\\\\to$1000 steps. $\\\\text{ISR}^*$ tells the opposite story, $0.642\\\\to0.132\\\\to0.003$ (214$\\\\times$ collapse). %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42\\n31:The cross-seed PR variance ($\\\\sigma{\\\\pm}47$\\\\,pp) shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes; PR/ISR$^*$: mean$\\\\pm$std of per-seed values. $^\\\\dagger$Seed~42. $\\\\ddagger$$\\\\text{ISR}^*$ omitted for ISR $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ because the raw mean is inflated by H7 gaming; premise-count-matched value is $1.06\\\\times$ random (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:goodhart}, Appendix~\\\\ref{app:detailed-results}). $^\\\\P$Includes H7 classifier false-negative correction (s44) and H8 premise-subset gaming (s42; Per-seed breakdown in Appendix Table~\\\\ref{tab:fol-detailed}.}\\n68:\\\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\\n69:The most striking number at 500 steps is H7: 69.7\\\\% of proved samples under ISR ($n{=}161/231$) vs.\\\\ \\\\emph{zero} under random ($0/256$); however, extended training to 1000 steps reveals random $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ converges to $100\\\\%$ H7 (seed~42), indicating ISR accelerates H7 onset rather than being its sole cause. %% registry: plan002 vs plan018 all seeds\\n70:H7---a single premise identical to the conclusion, giving $\\\\text{ISR}\\\\geq 0.5$ by construction (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:taxonomy-method})---is the canonical meta-level Goodhart move: the model satisfies the LOO definition directly\\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 3380 字符] ...\\n\\nking per seed; high-dose proxy weighting destabilizes training regardless of signal source; ISR produces diverse failure modes (reward collapse, H1 shortcutting, H7 meta-Goodhart) while random converges to H1-dominated hacking with sporadic H7 ($7\\\\%$ pooled, $0$--$7$ H7a per seed) (Appendix~\\\\ref{app:beta03}).\\n102:\\\\caption{Cross-logic results. \\\\textbf{Left}: FOL vs.\\\\ Prolog exec-only $\\\\text{ISR}^*$ across 3 seeds (s44 Prolog $0.387$ is a small-sample artifact, $n{=}21$). \\\\textbf{Right}: Prolog ISR-reward gaming-mode composition---all 3 seeds reach 0\\\\% non-hacking through distinct mechanisms.}\\n106:We replicate the FOL pipeline with Prolog/SWI-Prolog: (i) does ISR retain diagnostic value on a structurally different logic; (ii) does ISR-as-reward reproduce meta-level Goodhart?\\n109:\\\\textbf{Prolog exec-only: ISR catches redundancy across logics.}\\n110:Two of three seeds (s42, s43) show $\\\\text{ISR}^{*}{\\\\approx}0$ despite moderate-to-high PR (0.83, 0.56). %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42, plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3\\n112:This is a different failure semantics from FOL exec-only (conclusion embedding), but $\\\\text{ISR}^{*}$ flags both.\\n113:We are careful to distinguish: exec-only \\\\emph{low} ISR signals \\\\emph{redundancy}; ISR-rewarded \\\\emph{high} ISR signals \\\\emph{metric gaming} (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:taxonomy-method})---the same number does not license the same reasoning-quality conclusion.\\n115:\\\\textbf{Prolog ISR-as-reward: universal Goodhart.}\\n117:Seed~42: clean H7 tautologies ($\\\\text{ISR}^{*}{=}1.0$, all $50/100$ proved). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42\\n129:\\\\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples; exec-only and SFT: seed~42; ISR and Random bars: pooled across 3 seeds, per-seed breakdown in Appendix Table~\\\\ref{tab:fol-detailed}). ISR converts H1 into more extreme H7 without reducing total hacking; SFT shows near-zero hacking.}\\n139:\\\\textbf{Mode} & \\\\textbf{Exec} & \\\\textbf{ISR$_{.1}$} & \\\\textbf{Rnd$_{.1}$} & \\\\textbf{SFT} & \\\\textbf{Prolog$_{\\\\text{ISR}}$} \\\\\\\\\\n153:\\\\caption{Eight hacking modes across conditions (\\\\% of proved samples; exec-only/SFT: seed~42; ISR/Random: pooled across 3 seeds). H7 includes subtypes H7a (text-match, $n{=}149$) and H7b (obfuscated tautology, $n{=}12$; s44 only). Cells with seed identifiers indicate the mode dominates that seed. $^\\\\sharp$Prolog row pools strict+functional (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).}\\n158:Three are quantifiable across conditions: \\\\textbf{H1} (conclusion embedding) is GRPO-universal at $68$--$100\\\\%$ regardless of reward type; \\\\textbf{H7} (conclusion duplication) is ISR-accelerated at 500 steps ($69.7\\\\%$ combined vs.\\\\ $0\\\\%$ under random), though random $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ converges to $100\\\\%$ H7 by 1000 steps (seed~42); \\\\textbf{H8} (premise-subset gaming) appears under both exec-only ($6\\\\%$) and ISR ($5\\\\%$) conditions (Appendix~\\\\ref{app:h8}).\\n159:H7 splits into \\\\textbf{H7a} (standard, text-match detectable; $65\\\\%$ of ISR proved) and \\\\textbf{H7b} (obfuscated tautology; $5\\\\%$): H7b samples use vacuous quantifier wrapping ($11/12$) or structural simplification ($1/12$) to evade the text-match classifier while remaining logically equivalent, with $10/12$ achieving $\\\\text{ISR}{\\\\approx}0.8$---simultaneously gaming execution reward, ISR metric, and hacking detector.\\n160:The remaining five appear in specific cells: tautological collapse (exec-only s44, Prolog ISR s42), repetition (exec-only s43), contradiction explosion (exec-only at 1000 steps), complexity-without-utility (random $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ residual), and query-as-fact gaming (Prolog ISR s44).\\n163:Exec-only, ISR, and random conditions all show $\\\\geq 80\\\\%$ pooled hacking among proved samples; only SFT shows $92\\\\%$ non-hacking.\\n164:Across 2 logic types, 6 reward conditions, 3 seeds, and up to 1000 steps, no GRPO condition we tested produces a high-PR run that is also majority non-hacking; the closest is ISR $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ s42 at $\\\\text{PR}{=}81\\\\%$, $17\\\\%$ non-hacking, and even that residual is the premise-minimization artifact analyzed above.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.5}}"}

Cells with seed identifiers indicate the mode dominates that seed. $^\\\\sharp$Prolog row pools strict+functional (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).}\\n158:Three are quantifiable across conditions: \\\\textbf{H1} (conclusion embedding) is GRPO-universal at $68$--$100\\\\%$ regardless of reward type; \\\\textbf{H7} (conclusion duplication) is ISR-accelerated at 500 steps ($69.7\\\\%$ combined vs.\\\\ $0\\\\%$ under random), though random $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ converges to $100\\\\%$ H7 by 1000 steps (seed~42); \\\\textbf{H8} (premise-subset gaming) appears under both exec-only ($6\\\\%$) and ISR ($5\\\\%$) conditions (Appendix~\\\\ref{app:h8}).\\n159:H7 splits into \\\\textbf{H7a} (standard, text-match detectable; $65\\\\%$ of ISR proved) and \\\\textbf{H7b} (obfuscated tautology; $5\\\\%$): H7b samples use vacuous quantifier wrapping ($11/12$) or structural simplification ($1/12$) to evade the text-match classifier while remaining logically equivalent, with $10/12$ achieving $\\\\text{ISR}{\\\\approx}0.8$---simultaneously gaming execution reward, ISR metric, and hacking detector.\\n160:The remaining five appear in specific cells: tautological collapse (exec-only s44, Prolog ISR s42), repetition (exec-only s43), contradiction explosion (exec-only at 1000 steps), complexity-without-utility (random $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ residual), and query-as-fact gaming (Prolog ISR s44).\\n163:Exec-only, ISR, and random conditions all show $\\\\geq 80\\\\%$ pooled hacking among proved samples; only SFT shows $92\\\\%$ non-hacking.\\n164:Across 2 logic types, 6 reward conditions, 3 seeds, and up to 1000 steps, no GRPO condition we tested produces a high-PR run that is also majority non-hacking; the closest is ISR $\\\\beta{=}0.1$ s42 at $\\\\text{PR}{=}81\\\\%$, $17\\\\%$ non-hacking, and even that residual is the premise-minimization artifact analyzed above.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.5}}"

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Experiments}\\n\\\\label{sec:experiments}\\n\\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: ISR as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); ISR as a training reward, where it exhibits meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and STA as a cross-logic protocol (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:crosslogic}).\\nA hacking taxonomy synthesizes the modes observed across conditions (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Setup}\\\\label{sec:setup}\\n\\nWe train Qwen3-4B~\\\\citep{qwen3} with GRPO~\\\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$, lr $10^{-6}$, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) over seeds $\\\\{42,43,44\\\\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\\\\citep{folio} subset held constant across all conditions; the SFT baseline is trained with NLL on gold formalizations.\\\\footnote{All experiments use \\\\texttt{data/train\\\\_500\\\\_seed42.jsonl}; seeds control initialization and rollout sampling only.}\\nHeld-out evaluation uses 100 FOLIO examples (\\\\texttt{proofwriter\\\\_fol\\\\_100.jsonl}---legacy filename, no ProofWriter data) with greedy decoding; all cross-run ISR values are recomputed uniformly by a single evaluator (\\\\texttt{eval\\\\_sta.py}) so training-log ISR is never compared across runs.\\nWe report prove rate (PR), proved-only ISR ($\\\\text{ISR}^*$), hacking-mode rates from a two-stage classifier (strict text matching for H1/H7 plus functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\\\\text{necessary}}$ distribution; \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:taxonomy-method}), and a \\\\emph{non-hacking} (N-H) rate---the residual of proved samples matching none of the eight cataloged patterns, a necessary but not sufficient condition for semantic correctness.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\\n\\\\label{sec:diagnostic}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\\\\%$\\\\to$93\\\\%$\\\\to$96\\\\% while proved-only ISR falls $0.642{\\\\to}0.132{\\\\to}0.003$ over 100$\\\\to$500$\\\\to$1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\\\\% of proved samples have $\\\\text{ISR}{=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\\n\\\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\\nFigure~\\\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces exec-only GRPO (seed~42) at 100, 500, 1000 steps.\\nPR rises $32\\\\%\\\\to93\\\\%\\\\to96\\\\%$, a trajectory execution-only evaluation would read as successful optimization; $\\\\text{ISR}^*$ tells the opposite story, $0.642\\\\to0.132\\\\to0.003$ (214$\\\\times$ collapse). %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42\\nBy 500 steps, 87.1\\\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion (H1); by 1000 steps the embedding rate saturates at 100\\\\% and mean component count more than doubles ($6.58\\\\to18.55$) as the model emits contradictory or vacuous premises that trivially derive any conclusion.\\nExtended training, lacking a structural signal, \\\\emph{worsens} formalization quality while improving the headline number.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge into qualitatively different failure modes despite shared data, hyperparameters, and reward (Appendix~\\\\ref{app:detailed-results}): s42 reaches $\\\\text{PR}{=}93\\\\%$ via conclusion embedding ($\\\\text{H1}{=}87\\\\%$); s43 collapses to $\\\\text{PR}{=}13\\\\%$ via repetition (87\\\\% of rollouts truncated); s44 reaches $\\\\text{PR}{=}98\\\\%$ via tautological collapse ($\\\\text{H7}{=}100\\\\%$, mean $n{=}2.04$). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44\\nThe cross-seed PR variance ($\\\\sigma{\\\\pm}47$\\\\,pp) shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes; 2.2 & \\\\\\\\\\n  & 44 & 0.23 & 0 & 0 & 100 & 0 & \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\\n  & \\\\multicolumn{2}{l}{3-seed mean$\\\\pm$std} & \\\\makebox[0pt][l]{$0.54{\\\\pm}0.35$} & & \\\\makebox[0pt][l]{$0.09{\\\\pm}0.09$} & & \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Early-Training Hacking Analysis (100-Step Checkpoint)}\\n\\\\label{app:100step}\\n\\nTo control for the prove-rate confound in our SFT comparison (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:diagnostic}), we analyze the exec-only checkpoint at 100 training steps (seed~42), where PR${=}32$\\\\%---higher than SFT (13.0\\\\%, 3-seed pooled) but still early in training.\\nOf the 32 proved samples, 9 (28.1\\\\%) exhibit H7 hacking (single premise identical to conclusion) and 1 (3.1\\\\%) exhibits H1 (conclusion embedded among premises), yielding a total hacking rate of 31.2\\\\%.\\nBy contrast, SFT at comparable or lower PR shows 7.7\\\\% hacking (1/13 proved samples).\\nThis demonstrates that reward hacking emerges early in GRPO training---well before the prove-rate plateau---and is not simply an artifact of having more proved samples available for exploitation.\\nThe 100-step hacking rate (31.2\\\\%) is already substantially above SFT (7.7\\\\%), supporting the claim that hacking is associated with RL optimization rather than prove-rate level alone.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{$O(1)$ vs.\\\\ $O(n)$ ISR Ablation}\\n\\\\label{app:o1-ablation}\\n\\nTo justify the per-clause LOO design (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:sta}), we train a variant that replaces the $O(n)$ ablation with a cheaper $O(1)$ binary signal: $\\\\text{fast\\\\_ISR}(x) = 1$ if $x$ is proved \\\\emph{and} the conclusion is not provable under empty premises (tautology check), $0$ otherwise.\\nThe reward becomes $r = 0.5\\\\,r_{\\\\text{exec}} + 0.5\\\\,\\\\text{fast\\\\_ISR}$.\\nAfter 500 steps (seed~42), this variant achieves $\\\\text{PR}{=}63\\\\%$ with $\\\\text{ISR}^*{=}0.007$---functionally zero---compared to $\\\\text{ISR}^*{=}0.132$ under standard exec-only and $\\\\text{ISR}^*{=}0.42$ under ISR $\\\\beta{=}0.1$.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{ISR signal vs.\\\\ training dynamics.}\\nAt the \\\\emph{ISR signal level}, the $O(1)$ variant is indistinguishable from exec-only: both produce near-zero ISR because models emit redundant premise copies whose individual removal never changes the proof outcome.\\nAt the \\\\emph{training level}, the two conditions are non-equivalent: the $O(1)$ tautology check penalizes formalizations where the conclusion is provable without any premises, a constraint absent from exec-only.\\nThis confound depresses prove rate by 30 percentage points ($63\\\\%$ vs.\\\\ $93\\\\%$ exec-only), making it impossible to attribute the difference purely to the ISR signal.\\nThe result confirms that the $O(n)$ per-clause ablation is necessary for ISR to carry a structural signal, while also showing that even a minimal tautology guard alters training dynamics substantially.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}

\\\\makebox[0pt][l]{$0.09{\\\\pm}0.09$} & & \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Early-Training Hacking Analysis (100-Step Checkpoint)}\\n\\\\label{app:100step}\\n\\nTo control for the prove-rate confound in our SFT comparison (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:diagnostic}), we analyze the exec-only checkpoint at 100 training steps (seed~42), where PR${=}32$\\\\%---higher than SFT (13.0\\\\%, 3-seed pooled) but still early in training.\\nOf the 32 proved samples, 9 (28.1\\\\%) exhibit H7 hacking (single premise identical to conclusion) and 1 (3.1\\\\%) exhibits H1 (conclusion embedded among premises), yielding a total hacking rate of 31.2\\\\%.\\nBy contrast, SFT at comparable or lower PR shows 7.7\\\\% hacking (1/13 proved samples).\\nThis demonstrates that reward hacking emerges early in GRPO training---well before the prove-rate plateau---and is not simply an artifact of having more proved samples available for exploitation.\\nThe 100-step hacking rate (31.2\\\\%) is already substantially above SFT (7.7\\\\%), supporting the claim that hacking is associated with RL optimization rather than prove-rate level alone.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{$O(1)$ vs.\\\\ $O(n)$ ISR Ablation}\\n\\\\label{app:o1-ablation}\\n\\nTo justify the per-clause LOO design (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:sta}), we train a variant that replaces the $O(n)$ ablation with a cheaper $O(1)$ binary signal: $\\\\text{fast\\\\_ISR}(x) = 1$ if $x$ is proved \\\\emph{and} the conclusion is not provable under empty premises (tautology check), $0$ otherwise.\\nThe reward becomes $r = 0.5\\\\,r_{\\\\text{exec}} + 0.5\\\\,\\\\text{fast\\\\_ISR}$.\\nAfter 500 steps (seed~42), this variant achieves $\\\\text{PR}{=}63\\\\%$ with $\\\\text{ISR}^*{=}0.007$---functionally zero---compared to $\\\\text{ISR}^*{=}0.132$ under standard exec-only and $\\\\text{ISR}^*{=}0.42$ under ISR $\\\\beta{=}0.1$.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{ISR signal vs.\\\\ training dynamics.}\\nAt the \\\\emph{ISR signal level}, the $O(1)$ variant is indistinguishable from exec-only: both produce near-zero ISR because models emit redundant premise copies whose individual removal never changes the proof outcome.\\nAt the \\\\emph{training level}, the two conditions are non-equivalent: the $O(1)$ tautology check penalizes formalizations where the conclusion is provable without any premises, a constraint absent from exec-only.\\nThis confound depresses prove rate by 30 percentage points ($63\\\\%$ vs.\\\\ $93\\\\%$ exec-only), making it impossible to attribute the difference purely to the ISR signal.\\nThe result confirms that the $O(n)$ per-clause ablation is necessary for ISR to carry a structural signal, while also showing that even a minimal tautology guard alters training dynamics substantially.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"

2. Also tweaked the terminology from "signal content" to "signal source" around line 100.

**appendix.tex changes (3 lines added → 549 vs 546):**
1. Added a footnote marker in the table caption at line 394 to document an artifact in the s43-1000 ISR measurement
2. Updated the s43-1000 ISR value to a corrected 0.465 with the footnote reference (line 420)
3. Inserted a new row with pooled statistics across all three seeds for the 1000-step results (line ~426)

**registry changes:**
Looking at the registry modifications...

Verifying the numbers in the new experiments.tex paragraph... I'm verifying the pooled ISR values across the three scenarios—the corrected value of 0.465 for s43 gives a mean of 0.157, which matches the reported 0.16±0.27, so the table is using the corrected ISR rather than the raw measurement.

Now I'm noticing the registry shows isr_std dropped from 0.2472 to 0.15, which is a substantial shift. This suggests the ISR distribution across H7a proofs may have changed—some proofs with existential quantifier wrapping produce higher ISR values around 0.67 or 0.5 due to decomposition artifacts, while simpler structures yield lower values, and the overall standard deviation depends on how these are distributed. I'm noticing the field names and values shifted between registry reads — "isr" became "mean_isr" and the std dropped from 0.2472 to 0.15. This suggests the worker recalculated the standard deviation after correcting for the decomposition artifact on individual samples, which would legitimately reduce the variance in the distribution. The question is whether applying per-sample corrections and then recalculating the std is the right methodological approach here.

Looking at what the paper actually reports, the appendix shows the 3-seed pooled std of 0.27 (cross-seed variation), but doesn't display individual seed standard deviations anywhere. So the within-seed std of 0.15 in the registry is internal metadata that doesn't directly appear in the published results, which means any discrepancy here wouldn't affect the paper's claims.

I've verified the key updates: the 1000-step convergence paragraph in experiments.tex checks out, the ISR correction for s43 is documented in the appendix with a footnote explaining it as a decomposition artifact, and the registry reflects these corrected values. The main concern is that the registry notes a solver bug contributing +0.025 to the artifact, but this correction isn't actually applied to the reported 0.465 value—that's worth clarifying in the documentation.

Now I'm checking the paper's claim about the 3-seed pooled ISR at 1000 steps being 0.16±0.27. Working through the corrected values for s42, s43, and s44, the mean comes to 0.1567 which rounds to 0.16, confirming that figure is accurate.

For the standard deviation calculation, I'm verifying the variance across those three seeds works out to approximately 0.267, which rounds to 0.27 as stated in the paper. Both values check out.

Now I need to look more carefully at the registry's isr_std value of 0.15 — this appears to reflect the within-s43 standard deviation rather than the pooled value, and given that the 90 proved samples are all H7a tautological with single premises, the variance in ISR values would be relatively constrained since they all share that same structural property.

The decrease from 0.25 to 0.15 after correcting for the decompose_fol artifact makes directional sense — removing the extra components from the existentially-wrapped samples would bring their ISR values closer to the non-wrapped ones, reducing overall variance. I can't verify from the registry alone whether this was recomputed per-sample or manually adjusted, but it's a minor detail. The overall results still constitute a clean pass.

PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time.
